# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-29

| **[When Is Additional Retrieval Worthwhile? A Cost-Aware Study of Evidence Gain and Answer Reliability in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37358v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can benefit from more sophisticated retrieval, but extra retrieval may be unnecessary for many questions and gains in evidence retrieval may not translate into faithful or correct answers. We study a cost-aware, two-action retrieval policy that chooses between a dense-retrieval-plus-reranking baseline and a hybrid lexical--dense retriever. Rather than classifying baseline retrieval as simply successful or unsuccessful, we estimate the question-specific incremental evidence-retrieval benefit of hybrid retrieval using features available before the routing decision. A regularized linear model and decision threshold are selected using document-grouped cross-validation and frozen prior to held-out evaluation. On 60 previously unseen QASPER training-split articles (188 questions, 169 evidence-evaluable), the policy triggers hybrid retrieval on 46 questions (24.5\%) and obtains the same evidence Hit@10 as always-hybrid retrieval (78.70\%, versus 76.92\% for always-keep). Paired, paper-cluster bootstrap intervals do not establish a reliable utility advantage. We further compare generated answers from a 4-bit Qwen3-4B model under the three retrieval policies. Provisional answer-level assessments show no clear correctness or faithfulness improvement after partial review, despite the evidence-retrieval result. The generation assessments remain model-assisted and lack independent human validation. These results expose an important distinction between retrieving relevant evidence efficiently and improving the reliability of the generated answer. The work is presented as a reproducible empirical study, not a claim of statistically established answer-quality superiority.</p></details> |  |
| **[p-DCIM: A 28nm Probabilistic SRAM DCIM based Ising Machine for Near-Memory Context Selection in LLM-RAG Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37033v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a fabricated SRAM DCIM Ising machine that performs set-level context selection for large language model (LLM) with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). In RAG, the evidence-selection stage is increasingly delegated to repeated language-model reranking, which adds substantial energy to the inference pipeline. We reformulate evidence selection as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem and solve it on a dual-mode digital compute-in-memory (DCIM) macro: the same multiply-and-accumulate datapath computes the embedding-similarity scores that populate the QUBO coefficients and evaluates the per-spin Hamiltonian differences that drive the anneal. The annealing temperature is set by a digital plane-count control along with the SRAM pseudo-read random bit flips, giving graded cooling entirely from on-chip digital configuration. The macro is taped-out in a 28 nm process [0.16 mm2, 0.41 mW] and is proposed to be integrated as part of the logic base-die in hybrid high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and high-bandwidth flash (HBF) stacks in a GPU package, where only the selected passage identifiers return to the host. Replacing the language-model selection pass with the near-memory Ising solver yields a macro normalized selection-energy estimate more than six orders of magnitude lower than the LLM-based baseline and reduces the evaluated total energy per query by 67%, while maintaining comparable token-level F1 on the evaluated examples.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAG-CSI: Global Local Retrieval via Joint State and Dynamics Matching for CSI Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36881v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Channel state information (CSI) prediction forecasts future channel states from historical observations, supporting transceiver design and resource allocation with reduced measurement overhead. However, existing deep learning predictors mainly encode channel knowledge in model parameters, which can limit their ability to represent diverse patterns of channel dynamics across scenes. In this paper, we propose RAG-CSI, a retrieval-enhanced Transformer framework that augments prediction with relevant historical channel knowledge. Specifically, we construct a global--local CSI knowledge store containing representative observations across scenes and historical observations from the current serving scene. We further develop joint state--dynamics retrieval (JSDR), which jointly characterizes channel states, phase dynamics, and amplitude dynamics to retrieve relevant CSI sequences. A Transformer predictor then uses cross-attention to fuse the retrieved knowledge with the current CSI history and predict future CSI with limited additional model parameters. Experiments on a multi-scene CSI dataset show overall improvements exceeding 10\% across all five evaluation metrics over the best baseline for each metric, including a 15.42\% reduction in normalized mean squared error.</p></details> |  |
| **[Governed Sovereign RAG for Public Administration: Tripartite Epistemic Routing, Facet-Guided Clarification, and Deterministic Numeric Verification in Citizen Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36561v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying conversational assistants at government service desks faces a tension between regulatory certainty (zero tolerance for invented fees), data sovereignty prohibiting commercial clouds, and high rates of underspecified or out-of-jurisdiction citizen queries. We present and evaluate a governed, sovereign Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) architecture deployed on the official portal of the State of Tlaxcala, Mexico (tlaxcaladigital.gob.mx), covering 1,143 procedures and a decoupled directory of 924 offices. The system governs interactions via three pillars: (1) a two-stage tripartite epistemic gate (clarify_policy and a Boltzmann distribution over AnyJev) arbitrating among answering (SPECIFIC), facet-guided clarification (CLARIFY), and abstaining (ABSTAIN); (2) a post-generation deterministic numeric verifier (verify_numeric_guardrail) with SHA-256 cache invalidation ensuring every generated fee or validity figure belongs to the retrieved official record; and (3) an empirical evaluation across 380 organic production traces (8 weeks) and a 200-query stratified benchmark (5-fold CV: 86.41% +/- 7.50%). In production, 89.47% of traffic is resolved at Level 0 via deterministic templates without free generation (global p50 = 0.665 s; direct L0 p50 = 0.445 s; slow-tail p95 = 30.412 s under CPU queuing), with 0/40 fiscal amount hallucinations observed in Level 1 (95% bound <= 8.81%). On the benchmark, the system achieves 86.50% strict Top-1 accuracy (+10.00 pp over a binary neural gate, p = 0.00453, p_Holm = 0.01172), pairing a 93.00% conditional safe upper bound with a 70.87% dropout-adjusted effective resolution after accounting for 53.33% citizen drop-off in clarification menus.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 5 figures, 6 tables. Deployed in production at SEDECO/CEMERT, State of Tlaxcala, Mexico</p></details> |
| **[QESChunker: A Single Objective Unifies Overlapping and Non-Overlapping Chunking for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36557v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chunking is a fundamental preprocessing step in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), yet most existing chunkers rely primarily on heuristics. We introduce the Question--Evidence-Supervised Chunker (QESChunker), to our knowledge the first method for constructing static chunk indices from question--evidence supervision. QESChunker constructs document-specific chunk indices by optimizing over human-annotated or LLM-generated question--evidence pairs, without access to held-out test queries. We formulate non-overlapping and overlapping index construction through a unified question--evidence utility-maximization objective, turning chunking into a supervised combinatorial optimization problem. Under the non-overlap constraint, the objective decomposes over chunks and can be optimized exactly in polynomial time by dynamic programming. When overlap is allowed, the objective becomes monotone submodular; exact optimization is NP-hard, but our greedy achieves a $(1-1/\sqrt{e})$-approximation. Across two self-constructed and three public datasets, both variants outperform existing chunking methods in complete-evidence recall in nearly all evaluated settings, under the same overlap regime and a fixed hybrid retriever. These results recast chunking as supervised, budget-aware index construction for evidence retrieval.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 8 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[ScalableRAG: High-Quality RAG with Exact Corpus Operations at Minimal Ingestion Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25135v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>RAG systems face a capability-cost gap: lightweight retrievers locate evidence but expose no operands for exact corpus-level operations (counts, intersections, aggregations), while knowledge ingestion systems obtain them only through expensive ingestion. ScalableRAG closes this gap with a persistent typed-set workspace: an agent builds named document and value sets at query time and composes them through set algebra, typed extraction, aggregation, and diagnostics. Zero-Ingestion ScalableRAG needs no preprocessing, not even a vector index. It matches the leading baseline on four conventional QA benchmarks (74.75 versus 74.64) and on aggregative Hotels-50 beats every evaluated system, scoring 71.74: 36.23 above the leading baseline (SRAG), and 40.22 points above A-RAG. Removing the workspace drops Hotels-50 to 38.4. On Hotels-350, the partition SRAG itself reports on, ScalableRAG leads SRAG by 9.3 points. Limited-Ingestion ScalableRAG adds a lightweight index and is the only evaluated system above 80 judge score at 5M documents, at 13 to 16 seconds per question and under 20 minutes ingestion; at 1M it scores 84.5 to A-RAG's 80.0 with 16x lower latency and 0.5% of its ingestion time. ScalableRAG combines minimal ingestion, conventional-QA parity, exact corpus operations, and million-document viability; no evaluated alternative provides all four. Our code is available at https://github.com/cohesity/ScalableRAG .</p></details> |  |
| **[OA-RAG: Ontology-based Agentic RAG for Faithful LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36335v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) perform well across many real-world tasks but remain prone to hallucination, limiting their reliability in domains such as biomedicine and healthcare, where answers must be grounded in established knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates this problem by incorporating external information, but often overlooks logical relationships and relies heavily on the LLM's own reasoning, which can compromise answer faithfulness. We propose OA-RAG, an agent that integrates formal reasoning with LLM reasoning, drawing on both the logical structure and informal annotations of domain ontologies. For each answer, OA-RAG reports the ontology observations used as evidence and a model-assessed grounding level (entailed, supported, related, or unrelated) that characterises the strength of that evidence. Across benchmarks derived from three large-scale ontologies and six real-world medical question-answering tasks, OA-RAG achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated ontology-fine-tuned and popular RAG methods, with particularly strong results on reasoning-intensive tasks. The reported grounding levels also offer a useful indication of answer reliability, with stronger grounding generally associated with higher accuracy. Finally, our results demonstrate the value of ontologies as knowledge sources: OA-RAG is competitive with, and sometimes outperforms, domain-specific RAG systems that draw on sources such as textbooks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Geometric Gain Graph: Zero-Token Graph Construction for Multi-Hop RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19622v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems struggle with multi-hop reasoning due to a fundamental inability to balance relevance and novelty. Traditional dense retrievers are constrained by surface-level matching, falling into a homogenized "Similarity Trap". Conversely, emerging Graph RAGs attempt to explore novel documents via heuristic entity extraction, but they introduce prohibitive Large Language Model (LLM) overhead and easily drift into off-topic "Novelty Traps". To fundamentally resolve this contradiction, we propose Geometric Gain Graph (G$^3$RAG), the novel zero-token graph construction paradigm for RAG. G$^3$RAG completely discards the expensive heuristic extraction by LLMs, aiming instead to directly quantify the information gain between nodes through the native document feature space. To this end, we designed a concise edge weight criterion, $\cosθ\cdot \sinθ$, which naturally maps the trade-off between relevance and novelty into a geometric measurement of directional consistency and orthogonality in the vector space, thereby driving the token-free topological construction of the graph. Furthermore, we introduce a topological penalty to suppress the excessive connectivity of high-frequency hubs, forcing the transient diffusion process toward long-tail peripheral nodes that carry critical indirect evidence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, while entirely eliminating LLM token costs during graph construction, G$^3$RAG significantly outperforms state-of-the-art Graph RAG baselines on complex multi-hop QA benchmarks. The code is available at: https://github.com/deeplearning-LI/G3RAG</p></details> |  |
| **[Reading Beyond OCR Noise: Robust Cross-Lingual RAG for Legislative Archives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35240v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>India's historical public archives present severe computer vision and document image analysis challenges: decades of parliamentary records survive only as degraded, multi-column scanned pages featuring mixed scripts, complex layouts, and dense visual noise where OCR failure is the primary obstacle to information retrieval. We present an end-to-end framework that couples vision-based layout segmentation and multi-script OCR with an agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline. Our approach specifically addresses how visual degradation and layout fragmentation propagate into text extraction errors, using an iterative agentic loop to navigate around OCR noise and layout ambiguities before generating answers. Evaluated on the 200,000-page Punjab Vidhan Sabha archive, our system demonstrates robust handling of historical document degradation, outperforming standard text-only baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[CalibraRL: Confidence-Calibrated Reinforcement Learning for Mitigating Hallucinations in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35142v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) often produce plausible but incorrect answers, motivating reinforcement learning methods that reward correct answers, penalize incorrect answers, and allow abstention. However, fixed outcome-based rewards do not distinguish incorrect responses with different confidence levels or account for differences in answering reliability across queries. Consequently, such rewards may inadequately penalize overconfident errors and fail to discourage unnecessary abstention. We propose CalibraRL, a reinforcement learning framework that incorporates response confidence and query-level answering evidence into reward design. For non-abstaining responses, CalibraRL combines correctness-based utility with a Brier-based penalty that encourages agreement between confidence and correctness. For abstentions, it estimates conditional answering reliability using a Beta posterior derived from the correctness of non-abstaining responses within each sampled group. The abstention reward increases with the posterior probability that the model's accuracy on non-abstaining responses is below a predefined threshold. CalibraRL reuses training rollouts without requiring additional annotations indicating whether the model knows the answer to each question. Experiments on three QA datasets demonstrate that CalibraRL improves answer precision and reduces hallucinations compared with strong baselines. Further analysis shows that CalibraRL reduces mean expected calibration error (ECE) by 30.8\% across two backbone models and three datasets, highlighting its potential to improve the reliability of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) in web search.</p></details> | 12 pages, 10 figures |
| **[Hallucination-Aware Multi-Agent RAG via Internal Attention Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35056v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in retrieved evidence, but its outputs often contain claims unsupported by that evidence. Recent multi-agent RAG systems decompose the pipeline into cooperative decisions, yet they typically optimize all agents under a shared task reward such as EM or F1, which measures answer overlap rather than faithfulness to evidence. We propose a hallucination-aware multi-agent RAG framework that combines the task reward with an internal hallucination-risk signal computed from the generator's attention-head norms. Three role-specific LoRA adapters, a query planner, an evidence selector, and an answer generator, share a frozen backbone. A lightweight critic is trained once on TruthfulQA truthful/false pairs and then kept frozen, providing a complementary risk score that is added to the shared reward during cooperative PPO training. Across three backbones (Qwen3-8B, Llama3-8B, Llama2-13B) and three benchmarks (RAGTruth, HotpotQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA), our method improves task performance and reduces hallucination rates over the strongest multi-agent baseline. Ablations and critic diagnostics show that the TruthfulQA-calibrated signal provides useful reward shaping that transfers across RAG tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Findi...</summary><p>Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Adaptive Retrieval, Selective Answers: Evaluating Conformal Factuality in Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34872v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can improve factual grounding without guaranteeing that every generated claim is supported. Previous work has studied claim-level conformal factuality in single-stage RAG and, more recently, multi-hop RAG. We examine its behavior in agentic RAG, where a language-model policy adaptively issues follow-up retrieval queries or terminates retrieval before response generation. We retain the retrieval-based relevance-scoring formulation and post-generation conformal filtering and evaluate six model-dataset configurations: GPT-6 Luna and Llama 3.1 8B on HotpotQA, Natural Questions, and TriviaQA. Each configuration uses 60 fixed questions, 100 seeded 30/30 calibration-test partitions, five conformal target levels, and a no-conformal-prediction baseline. Across the six configurations, higher target levels are accompanied by higher observed full-support rates and reduced answer availability. At the 95% target, non-empty response coverage ranges from 17.23% to 60.17%, claim retention from 10.72% to 43.16%, and full support among non-empty outputs from 89.14% to 95.56%. We formulate the adaptive retrieval trajectory explicitly and characterize the reliability-informativeness trade-off when conformal factuality is applied after agent termination.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 1 figure, 5 tables. Code: https://github.com/aimalrehman92/conformal-rag-agentic</p></details> |
| **[Logic Hijacking of RAG: Single-Document Poisoning via Local Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 3 figures, 14 tables</p></details> |
| **[Can System-1-Style Decision Models Replace Dedicated Rerankers in RAG? A Systems Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34816v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems often use a dedicated reranker to choose which retrieved candidates reach the language model. Reranking improves relevance but adds latency, so teams look for faster alternatives. System-1style decision models make direct, structured predictions instead of generating long responses. Could one provide comparable reranking quality with less inference overhead? We first screen 21 open-weight models on 11 benchmarks, then compare a promising decision model with a dedicated reranker on identical candidate lists. The default decision-model setup fails on many long candidate sets, so scoring only the requests it completes gives a misleading ranking. Limiting candidate text length largely restores reliability and makes some configurations competitive. A frozen evaluation on new retrieval data supports the reliability and ranking results, but confirms the latency requirement for only one of the two selected configurations. Across workloads, which model ranks better changes, and a tuned reranker remains faster and uses far less GPU memory. A decision model can perform competitive reranking, but replacing a reranker requires weighing quality, latency and memory together, and teams should tune the reranker they already run first.</p></details> |  |
| **[LatentRAG: Latent Reasoning and Retrieval for Efficient Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06285v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Single-step retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) provides an efficient way to incorporate external information for simple question answering tasks but struggles with complex questions. Agentic RAG extends this paradigm by replacing single-step retrieval with a multi-step process, in which the large language model (LLM) acts as a search agent that generates intermediate thoughts and subqueries to iteratively interact with the retrieval system. This iterative process incurs substantial latency due to the autoregressive generation of lengthy thoughts and subqueries. To address this limitation, we propose LatentRAG, a novel framework that shifts both reasoning and retrieval from discrete language space to continuous latent space. Unlike existing explicit methods that generate natural language thoughts or subqueries token-by-token, LatentRAG produces latent tokens for thoughts and subqueries directly from the hidden states in a single forward pass. We align LLMs with dense retrieval models in the latent space, enabling retrieval over latent subquery tokens and supporting end-to-end joint optimization. To improve transparency and encourage semantically meaningful latent representations, we incorporate a parallel latent decoding mechanism that translates latent tokens back into natural language. Extensive experiments on seven benchmark datasets show that LatentRAG achieves performance comparable to explicit agentic RAG methods while reducing inference latency by approximately 90%, substantially narrowing the latency gap with traditional single-step RAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>Neura...</summary><p>Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), 2026</p></details> |
| **[CoinRAG: Contextualized Information Nugget KV Cache Reuse for Long-Context RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07458v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent optimization studies on Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) have exploited chunk-level KV cache reuse to avoid processing long retrieved contexts for higher efficiency, while significant information redundancy and noise still remain in the coarse-grained chunks. This paper optimizes the Pareto frontier under low prefill latency constraints while maximizing accuracy by proposing CoinRAG (Contextualized Information Nugget KV Cache Reuse for Long-Context RAG). The name metaphorically reflects our core mechanism: much like assembling small tokens (or "coins") to accumulate a larger value, CoinRAG compositionally reuses offline-computed, fine-grained nugget caches to form a learned contextual representation efficiently in a more semantically relevant but compact manner. Specifically, instead of full-chunk encoding, CoinRAG identifies query-relevant semantic units within retrieved chunks through two-stage retrieval and seamlessly assembles their sliced KV representations with a chunk-level context. CoinRAG can be integrated with CacheBlend's selective recomputation for further accuracy gains under the same latency budget. Extensive evaluations on LongBench multi-hop question answering tasks demonstrate that CoinRAG substantially shortens the active context and outperforms the other baselines with a new Pareto frontier and an average 5.3% relative improvement in answer quality (F1) under a standard fast prefill latency budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[GEMS-RAG: Graph-grounded Engineering Multimodal Standards Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33277v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Engineering standards are challenging knowledge sources for large language models (LLMs) because they are hierarchical, multimodal, and densely connected through figures, tables, cross-references, and normative rules. Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) methods often rely on unstructured text retrieval and struggle with questions requiring joint reasoning over textual provisions, visual specifications, and document structure. We propose GEMS-RAG (Graph-grounded Engineering Multimodal Standards Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a framework for grounded reasoning over engineering standards. GEMS-RAG combines hierarchical parsing, fine-grained visual entity extraction, multimodal knowledge graph construction, and graph-grounded hybrid retrieval. Instead of treating figures as monolithic images, it decomposes them into meaningful visual entities linked to textual provisions, engineering concepts, and regulatory rules. During inference, dense, sparse, and visual retrieval are combined with graph expansion to identify relevant evidence across text, figures, and document structure. We evaluate GEMS-RAG on the Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices (MUTCD), a large engineering standard containing egulations, technical drawings, and standardized traffic-control devices. Results show that graph-grounded multimodal retrieval improves retrieval effectiveness and question-answering performance over text-only, multimodal, and graph-based RAG baselines. Beyond MUTCD, GEMS-RAG offers a general framework for reasoning over engineering codes, technical manuals, regulatory documents, and scientific standards in safety-critical applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[From One Query to Many: A Critical Survey of Privacy Accounting in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21245v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enables large language models to leverage external knowledge but introduces privacy risks when the retrieved corpus contains sensitive information. Differential privacy (DP) offers formal protection, yet interpreting and comparing privacy guarantees across existing DP-RAG methods remains challenging. In particular, a privacy budget reported for a single response does not directly characterize privacy loss over repeated queries, and even identical values of $\varepsilon$ may represent different guarantees depending on the protected data unit, accounting horizon, and observable outputs. This survey critically examines how privacy accounting evolves from single-query generation to multi-query RAG systems. We categorize existing approaches into query-time privatization, multi-query accounting, and one-time corpus privatization. We analyze their underlying assumptions and privacy--utility trade-offs. Rather than ranking methods by $\varepsilon$ alone, we propose a framework that interprets a DP-RAG guarantee along three dimensions: privacy granularity, accounting scope, and release boundaries. We conclude that an $\eps$ is a claim only together with the three slots: the protected unit, the accounting scope, and the release boundary.</p></details> |  |
| **[Is Complex Agentic Memory Always Needed? Naive RAG Is Stronger Than We Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30755v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM memory is widely used in long-term conversation understanding. Agentic memory systems often use mutation, structural organization, learned retrieval strategies, or other complex operations. Is this complexity always necessary? We study Raw-RAG, a naive RAG baseline using dense retrieval and reranking over raw history to supply evidence to a reader LLM. Across LoCoMo, LongMemEval, MEME, and MemoryAgentBench, Raw-RAG ranks first among the compared memory systems in six of seven benchmark-model settings, outperforming the strongest compared agentic memory system by 0.13-5.53 points. We investigate why raw-history retrieval remains competitive. Evidence analysis shows that its higher coverage of necessary evidence can offset lower utilization. Useful evidence includes both answer values and the relations and dependency paths needed to derive them. Even complete access to transformed memory stores leaves an accuracy gap relative to raw evidence, highlighting the importance of preserving raw information. These findings challenge the necessity of complex agentic memory for the evaluated conversation tasks and establish naive RAG as an essential baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>62 pa...</summary><p>62 pages, 5 figures. Includes appendices</p></details> |
| **[Compiling Autoregressive LLM Control into Policy-Exact Parallel Inference for Hierarchical RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30664v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models increasingly act as controllers in structured decision pipelines, yet their decisions are still executed by autoregressive text decoding. Hierarchical RAG is one such setting: at each frontier of a document tree, an LLM commits to $b$ of $k$ sections under an input-dependent action grammar, executed as either failure-prone free-form JSON or reliable but strictly serial grammar-constrained generation. We show that this serialization is unnecessary for bounded-horizon control: the reachable future forms a shallow action-prefix tree, which we compile --- one root prefill plus one tree-causal probe evaluate every prefix-conditioned action distribution with serial decoding's tokens, positions, and causal ancestry. A prefix-state lemma makes compiled control policy-exact in exact arithmetic: the same policy with a two-forward critical path and zero output tokens. One further probe pass certifies the exact order-marginalized MAP set. Across four document-disjoint splits and two scales on the primary MetaX device, compiled control matches the serial policy on $84$--$89\%$ of questions --- every instrumented divergence a floating-point near-tie --- and cuts control latency by $35$--$47\%$ versus token-wise serial decoding and $6$--$7\%$ versus an idealized chunked engine; an H800 repetition preserves the ordering.</p></details> | <details><summary>5 pag...</summary><p>5 pages, 2 figures, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[Operon: Incremental Construction of Ragged Data via Named Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16080v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern data processing workflows frequently encounter ragged data: multidimensional collections whose inner lengths vary and are revealed only at runtime. Ragged data is prevalent in domains throughout science and computing, yet existing workflow engines treat it as nested containers. This representation leaves shape semantics ambiguous, obscures task dependencies when upstream coordinates are not yet enumerated, and imposes coarse synchronization barriers that stall independent work across tasks. We argue that ragged data is fundamentally a flat function over a coordinate space, and that making this view explicit resolves all three difficulties at once. We realize this insight in Operon, a statically verifiable workflow engine built around a formalism of named dimensions with explicit dependency relations. Users declare pipelines in the Operon DSL by annotating data flow with dimension names, where the static checker verifies that every task does not include faulty iterations regardless of runtime lengths. At runtime, the system models in-progress arrays as partial functions over downward-closed coordinate domains, which provides a natural representation for partially-unknown data states, an exact notion of per-task indegree before all upstream coordinates are enumerated, and a sound basis for fault recovery without programmer intervention.</p></details> |  |
| **[From prompting to evidence-based translation: A RAG+prompt system for Japanese-Chinese translation and its pedagogical potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03387v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models perform well on high-resource pairs but are less reliable for Japanese-Chinese sentences containing noun-modifying clause constructions (NMCCs). This study evaluates a retrieval-augmented generation RAG+Prompt translation system that integrates linguistic analysis, embedding-based retrieval, prompt construction, and LLM generation without modifying the base model. The analysis module outputs A1 (inner vs. outer NMCC) and A2 (risk predictions: lexical choice/NMCC handling/word order/style/register); top-k = 5 similar Ja-Zh examples (L2 distance) and A1/A2 are inserted into an enhanced prompt. Using GPT-4o and a 66-sentence test set, we compare six knowledge-base sizes (0/100/200/500/1,000/2,000). First, retrieval reranking and context rewriting each span five or six endpoint families, so mechanisms do not identify protection outcomes. Second, only 17.8% of studies use content disclosure or reconstruction as their primary endpoint; under the frozen copyright/ownership relation label, 73.3% of records have corpus-use detection as their primary endpoint, and none have an emitted-expression primary endpoint. Third, most evaluations use general-purpose question-answering datasets and non-adaptive attacks rather than sensitive data and adaptive, multi-turn extraction. | **[FuseRAG: Fusing Structure and Evidence into Inference for Cost-Efficient Graph-based RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22818v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) exploits relationships among evidence to support multi-hop question answering. | **[Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18442v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. | **[Training-Free BioNEL-RAG: Advancing Biomedical Entity Linking through Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14789v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical Named Entity Linking (BioNEL) is the task of mapping mentions extracted from biomedical texts to canonical entities or concepts in the standardized knowledge base. Although existing methods have achieved remarkable performance in mapping accuracy, most rely on training with domain-specific datasets, which fundamentally limits their cross-domain adaptability. To address this limitation, we propose BioNEL-RAG, a novel training-free framework that combines retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with large language models (LLMs). Within this framework, the RAG module focuses on retrieving candidate entities, while the large language model module plays the role of selecting the most appropriate candidate for entity normalization, which is based on the analysis of the mention's context, with the aid of its remarkable text comprehension capabilities. Experimental results demonstrate that BioNEL-RAG achieves performance comparable to existing methods without requiring training on subdomain datasets, whereas these methods necessitate fine-tuning on specific subdomains such as genes, diseases, or chemical compounds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Frustratingly Easy Agentic RAG via Portable Skills: A Case Study in Customer Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14428v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise workflow automation typically requires retrieving a complete and accurate set of relevant information from large operational document corpora (SOPs, policy documents) given a specific scenario or request. In customer support (CS), for example, human specialists must determine the correct sequence of resolution steps for each case, a task that demands precise grounding in policy documentation. Meeting this bar in production typically requires complex document chunking, indexing, and retrieval pipelines, often layered with learned re-rankers, query rewriters, and bespoke agentic RAG infrastructure. We report a deployment case study of a simpler alternative: a portable skill consisting of a natural-language instruction, lightweight tools (a retrieval API, format-checking scripts), and a corpus enriched with an LLM-generated navigation layer, which is a structured "Wikipedia-style" index over the underlying documents. This agentic RAG approach is straightforward to implement (1-2 engineer days), portable across capable agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code), and performs strongly on the challenging task of CS resolution workflow generation, achieving 99.2% procedural equivalence to human-approved ground truth and 83.3% evidence sufficiency. It is also roughly an order of magnitude slower per case than our deployed production assistant (~90 s vs. ~8-9 s), so we claim no latency advantage. Our claim is scoped to that setting: a mature, well-curated corpus, a latency-tolerant workflow, and human review of every case. There, the skill did the work of a complex RAG stack, and maintenance became a matter of editing text and data rather than retraining models.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 Industry Track. 15 pages, 1 figure, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[ORCAGen: Orchestrating Context-Aware Malware Deception with RAG-Guided Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12415v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Malware defenses often remove or isolate suspicious programs as quickly as possible. While effective for containment, this approach can also waste an opportunity to observe attacker behavior and deploy targeted countermeasures. ORCAGen takes a different approach: it uses GenAI to build malware-specific deception playbooks offline, validates them before deployment, and enforces only the verified logic at runtime. ORCAGen combines Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with structured prompt engineering to generate both proof-of-concept (PoC) malware and corresponding deception orchestration code. A curated knowledge base (KB) of malware procedures and active defense strategies grounds the generation process, helping the LLM produce threat-specific and executable deception logic rather than generic or hallucinated outputs. The generated PoC malware provides a safe and reproducible way to test whether a deception strategy can disrupt, redirect, or suppress targeted malware behavior before the strategy is added to the runtime playbook. We evaluate ORCAGen across GPT-4o, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.5 Flash, Qwen3-Coder, and Claude Sonnet 4.5 using execution success, hallucination rate, refinement effort, deception effectiveness, and runtime overhead. The evaluation covers synthesized malware scenarios and 150 real-world malware samples across keyloggers, information stealers, and ransomware. Across the evaluated scenarios, GPT-5.5 required the fewest refinements and produced no observed hallucinated APIs, while Gemini 3.5 Flash achieved the lowest response time and runtime overhead. The results show that RAG-guided structured prompting can support the scalable construction of malware-specific deception playbooks that remain lightweight and deterministic during runtime enforcement.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at 2026 IEEE 8th International Conference on Trust, Privacy and Security in Intelligent Systems, and Applications (TPS-ISA)</p></details> |
| **[GLM-RAG: Graph Language Models for Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28397v2)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) over knowledge graphs requires retrievers that can effectively capture both graph structure and semantic information. Recent approaches have explored graph neural network (GNN)-based retrievers to model graph topology in multi-hop reasoning tasks. In this work, we introduce a GLM-based retriever and investigate the comparative strengths of GLM-based, GNN-based, and traditional vector-search-based retrievers in single- and multi-hop RAG settings, and with a particular focus on transferability to unseen domains. Our findings suggest that finetuned GLM retrievers generalize better out of domain, achieving SOTA on two multi-hop benchmarks. On in-domain multi-hop QA datasets they remain comparable to prior work, with promising scaling as parameters and subgraph coverage increase. GNN-based retrievers achieve higher graph coverage with an efficient training setup, whereas the vector-search baseline excels at single-hop datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to AACL-IJCNLP 2026, 9 pages, 19 figures</p></details> |
| **[RAG-Stress: Probing the Limits of Evidence Reliance in Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11183v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Following retrieved evidence does not guarantee factual correctness: misleading evidence can induce a model to replace an answer it previously gave correctly. Standard accuracy measures obscure this behavior by combining answer replacement with preexisting errors. The protocol holds the question and reference answer fixed, edits one assertion to support a designated incorrect answer, and crosses two source priority policies with three positions of the answer span within the evidence text. We measure misleading rate (MR) on each model's subset of questions answered correctly without retrieval, alongside clean accuracy on the full evaluation set. We evaluate fifteen systems spanning API models, open models, and search agents trained with reinforcement learning on TriviaQA-RC, HotpotQA, and SearchQA, with additional English and Chinese MedQA evaluations. Instructions that prioritize documents consistently produce higher MR than those permitting reliance on prior knowledge. Averaged over models and positions, the gap ranges from 10.9 to 13.5 percentage points across the three QA datasets. Mean MR follows End $>$ Beginning $>$ Middle under both policies, although individual models do not uniformly follow this ordering. A separate paired audit of 500 questions and two checkpoints supports increased harmful override without establishing a corresponding improvement in beneficial correction. These findings distinguish evidence adherence from factual reliability and motivate evaluating whether retrieved evidence preserves, replaces, or corrects a model's answers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Quantifying Retriever-Generator Alignment in RAG with Local Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21803v3)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems combine dense retrievers and language models to ground outputs in external documents. We present RAG-E, an end-to-end explainability framework that quantifies retriever-generator alignment through mathematically grounded attribution methods. Our approach adapts Integrated Gradients for retriever analysis, proposes a Monte Carlo-stabilized Shapley Value approximation for generator attribution, and introduces the Weighted Alignment between Retriever and Generator (WARG) metric to measure how closely the generator's document usage aligns with retriever rankings. Experiments on PopQA, QAMPARI, and TREC CAST datasets reveal substantial misalignment: depending on the model and setting, generators often ignore top-ranked documents and rely on documents ranked as less relevant. We show that WARG captures retriever-generator alignment better than Pearson and Spearman correlations and can serve as an indicator of RAG performance. RAG-E and WARG provide a practical framework for auditing this interaction, enabling more reliable and transparent RAG systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[EC-RAG: Event Chain Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08674v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current large video-language models (LVLMs) still face challenges when dealing with long videos, mainly because frames are often processed independently, making it difficult to capture temporal dependencies across events. Although retrieval-augmented approaches have been introduced to provide additional context, most of them operate at the frame or snippet level, which limits their ability to model how events evolve over time and relate to each other. In this paper, we propose Event Chain Retrieval-Augmented Generation (EC-RAG), a training-free framework that organizes video content into an explicit event chain before question answering. Instead of retrieving isolated frames or text segments, EC-RAG first partitions the video into semantically coherent segments, represents each segment using multi-modal signals, and then links them into a structured chain that preserves temporal order and captures inter-event relationships. Given a query, the system identifies relevant events within this chain and gathers supporting evidence from the associated modalities. Our approach offers several practical advantages: (i) event-level abstraction that better reflects how video content is naturally structured, enabling more reliable localization compared to frame-level retrieval; (ii) structured multi-modal fusion that aggregates speech, text, and visual cues at the event level, allowing complementary information to be more effectively utilized during reasoning; and (iii) plug-and-play compatibility with existing LVLM backbones, requiring no additional training or reliance on proprietary models. Experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench show that this event-centric design consistently outperforms frame-level retrieval baselines, highlighting the importance of modeling temporal structure for long-video understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 7 figures, 7 tables, including supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[RAG-PIBench: A Leakage-Aware Benchmark for Prompt-Injection Detection in Trustworthy RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08571v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are vulnerable to prompt-injection attacks embedded in retrieved content. | **[Agentic AutoRAG: RAG Pipeline Optimization through Reasoning-Driven Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08452v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a widely used approach for grounding large language models (LLMs) in external knowledge. Existing optimizers, from greedy search to Bayesian optimization, reduce each trial to an aggregate score and search without modeling why a configuration performed as it did, even though the retrieved chunks already provide evidence about whether each failure occurred during retrieval or after it. We introduce Agentic AutoRAG, an LLM-agent optimizer for multi-objective RAG hyperparameter optimization with retrieval-versus-generation failure attribution. It proposes configurations scored on a frozen exam from the corpus: after each trial a Diagnoser attributes each failed question to retrieval or generation, and a Proposer, grounded in a knowledge base of model rankings and pricing, selects the next configuration, weighing accuracy against cost to trace a Pareto frontier. On three multi-hop QA benchmarks it reaches higher LLM-judge accuracy than every baseline we compare, and within its first 10 trials it matches or beats the statistical baselines' full 30-trial judge accuracy. In its cost-aware mode on a real-world healthcare corpus it reaches a median exam accuracy of 77%, above the strongest baseline's 71.5%, at about 58% of that baseline's cost per query, and it matches that 71.5% at about 22% of the cost.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the Second Workshop for REsearch on Agent Language Models (REALM) at EMNLP 2026 and at the Machine Learning for Systems Workshop at NeurIPS 2026. 9 pages plus references and appendix (16 pages total), 4 figures, 6 tables. Code: https://github.com/Agentic-Systems-Lab/Agentic-AutoRAG</p></details> |
| **[Tool-calling retrieval versus vector RAG for a small Greek--English knowledge base: accuracy and robustness to how users type Greek](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08205v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assistants grounded in a small, frequently edited knowledge base can retrieve through tool calls to a live data interface or through vector retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We compare the two on KyGround, a benchmark of 198 questions drawn from the published records of a Greek--English agricultural platform on Kythera, Greece, with answers verified automatically against the records and each question posed in up to nine forms, including Greek without accents, in capitals and in three Latin-script (Greeklish) schemes. With Claude Haiku 4.5 as router and answer model, a reconstruction of the platform's tool agent answered 71.6\% of canonical Greek questions correctly and vector RAG 95.3\% (difference $-23.6$ percentage points, 95\% CI $-33.1$ to $-15.1$). Letting the router write the vector query changed nothing, and placing the whole knowledge base of about 26,000 tokens in the prompt reached 99.3\%. The tool agent's losses arose in retrieval. Its literal searches returned nothing when the router's arguments did not occur verbatim in a record, for example when it transliterated Greek into Latin script or combined words that occur in a record but not as one phrase, and the agent then abstained. Unaccented and capitalised questions cost the tool agent about 20 points and vector RAG at most 2; accent-insensitive search removed this loss, and matching stemmed tokens raised the tool agent to 83.8\% on canonical Greek. | **[DrugMCTS: a drug repurposing framework combining multi-agent, RAG and Monte Carlo Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07426v4)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in large language models have demonstrated considerable potential in scientific domains such as drug repositioning. However, their effectiveness remains constrained when reasoning extends beyond the knowledge acquired during pretraining. Conventional approaches, such as fine-tuning or retrieval-augmented generation, face limitations in either imposing high computational overhead or failing to fully exploit structured scientific data. The framework employs five specialized agents tasked with retrieving and analyzing molecular and protein information, thereby enabling structured and iterative reasoning. Extensive experiments on the DrugBank and KIBA datasets demonstrate that DrugMCTS achieves substantially higher recall and robustness compared to both general-purpose LLMs and deep learning baselines. Our results highlight the importance of structured reasoning, agent-based collaboration, and feedback-driven search mechanisms in advancing LLM applications for drug repositioning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking Knowledge Retrieval for Generation: A Survey on RAG Architectures and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01936v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable fluency and versatility across natural language tasks but remain fundamentally limited by their static knowledge and susceptibility to hallucinations, especially in domains requiring up to date or attribute grounded information. Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses these challenges by integrating external retrieval mechanisms with generative models, enabling dynamic, context aware generation grounded in verifiable data sources. This survey presents a comprehensive examination of RAG as a modular and evolving paradigm that enhances factual reliability, adaptability, and task alignment in LLM based systems. We formalize the RAG framework through its three foundational components retrieval, generation, and augmentation and survey state of the art methods spanning dense and sparse retrievers, fusion strategies, embedding optimizations, and reinforcement learning based retrieval policies. Anchored around four emerging axes efficiency, security, user centric interactivity, and complex reasoning we categorize recent innovations and highlight their implications for scalability, robustness, and personalization. The paper also reviews advances in evaluation protocols, domain specific applications, and architectural variants such as Naïve RAG, Advanced RAG, and Modular RAG. Finally, we identify persistent challenges and outline future directions aimed at advancing the integration of retrieval with LLMs for more grounded, interpretable, and controllable generation.</p></details> |  |

| **[Bounded Provisional Visibility: Controlling Poisoning Exposure in Continuously Ingested RAG Vector Stores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05826v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continuous ingestion can expose new retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) content to retrieval before vetting completes, creating a temporal attack surface that conventional admission decisions do not capture. We present a fail-closed provisional-visibility protocol that admits new content under a deadline, hides items whose verification has not committed in time, and supports lineage-scoped containment. The protocol bounds unvetted exposure by the configured visibility budget plus query-path enforcement delay; poisoning exposure remains conditional on verifier correctness. We evaluate the design on five workloads of encoded natural-language documents using an exact backend and Milvus. Under verifier backlog, the deadline reduced median poisoned retrievals from 34 (29--34) to 7 (6--7) relative to asynchronous admission without a deadline, with the same reduction in displaced clean results. Verify-before-visible avoided provisional exposure but delayed clean first visibility to 6.3 s, whereas provisional admission made content visible within 3 ms; expired clean items could nevertheless experience temporary availability gaps. Replaying decisions from a recipe-specific detector illustrated the protocol boundary: false promotions left poison visible, while false refusals excluded clean content from retrieval. Standalone Milvus tests characterized enforcement delay and concurrent HNSW retrieval on one node. These results clarify which guarantees come from the protocol and which outcomes depend on detector quality, while quantifying the freshness and availability trade-offs.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 2 figures, 1 table. Accepted at IEEE CBDCom 2026. Author accepted manuscript. Reproducibility artifact: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23152010</p></details> |

Archived nested and Q-only forecasts predict these allocations within 4.0\% and 3.5\%, respectively. Depth subsets establish no clear forecasting advantage beyond the two-trajectory audit. One-read variance penalties relative to the fitted optimum at the same token budget are 0--9.9\%, with substantial Pro uncertainty. Under recorded model fees, more questions beat more trajectories at search prices of \$0--1 per 1,000 requests; question-versus-read fee rankings remain unresolved. Temperature zero cuts answer disagreement from 14.3\% to 3.4\% while comparison precision stays similar. \par\medskip\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} Agentic RAG; Evaluation budget; Generalizability theory; Repeated sampling.</p></details> |  |

Previous studies have attempted to train LLMs on ethical standards, but the diversity and relativity of ethical norms make them difficult to fully internalize in model parameters. As an alternative, we introduce normative RAG, a retrieval-augmented approach that supports ethical judgment using external normative knowledge. Normative retrieval involves a distinct asymmetry between context rich narrative queries and generalized normative statements. Existing factual retrieval methods rely on query-only expansion into a document-like form, making them insufficient for resolving this asymmetry. Therefore, we propose Intermediate Representation-based Embedding Alignment (IREA), a bidirectional alignment method that maps both text types into a shared situation-behavior representation. This representation captures ethically salient contextual and behavioral information in a normalized form, reducing surface-level discrepancies and improving alignment in the embedding space. Experimental results show that IREA improves normative retrieval and downstream ethical judgment across multiple settings, demonstrating the effectiveness of bidirectional alignment for normative RAG.</p></details> |  |

Preprint</p></details> |
| **[Single-Round Vector RAG vs an LLM-Compiled Wiki: A Preregistered Comparison on a Small Multi-Domain Research Corpus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18490v3)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We preregistered a comparison of two ways to help an LLM answer questions over a small research corpus: single-round Vector RAG and an LLM-compiled markdown wiki browsed by a tool-using agent. Reader pairs also change order more often under compressed evidence than in raw replays across question halves. Row-level accounting explains how higher average accuracy and smaller upgrades coexist: compression rescues some wrong answers and damages some correct answers, with a different balance for each reader. We release ragscale to audit declared reader upgrades under raw and fixed compressed evidence. Code and data: https://github.com/aimsresearchlab/ragscale</p></details> |
| **[How To Train Your World Model: Fine-tuning vs RAG for LM-based World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02542v1)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models (WMs) simulate the transition dynamics of environments, enabling agents to plan over the consequences of their actions. We conduct a systematic evaluation across five diverse environments spanning embodied, web navigation and social settings, comparing fine-tuning and RAG-based approaches for LM-based world modelling. Our study reveals that fine-tuning often outperforms RAG, with fine-tuned WMs enabling agents to obtain higher rewards on 15/20 settings. While both construction paradigms benefit from additional and more diverse exploration, RAG-based approaches prove more data-efficient, and fine-tuning approaches disproportionately benefit from scaling the amount of experience collected. With a focus on RAG-based WMs, we devise a procedure that uses counterfactual intervention to estimate the error rate of the retrieval stage, and show that retrievers consistently surface suboptimal transitions from the experience buffer. Hoping to address this failing, we study a variety of query reformulation strategies, demonstrating that a hierarchical approach outperforms the traditional retrieval pipeline. Finally, we compose our findings into a hybrid world modelling system that parametrically captures core environment dynamics, while learning to rely on retrieval from an actively maintained memory store. Our hybrid system consistently outperforms other methods across multiple environments and models, showcasing the robustness of the approach and the applicability of our findings.</p></details> |  |
| **[On-Premises Multi-Course RAG Tutoring for Business Education: Hardware-Software Trade-offs in a Campus AI Tutor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02510v1)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Campus AI tutors based on retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) must ground answers in assigned course materials while keeping textbooks and student dialogue on institutional infrastructure. | **[A Matryoshka Hierarchical RAG for Efficient Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01767v1)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems for multi-hop Question Answering (QA) must balance retrieval quality with computational cost. An unknown group of nodes, no larger than a declared bound, may misreport both in this step and at query time. Existing methods assume every node is honest or protect only the calibration step. We observe that the honest nodes are the same in both steps. | **[GEPA-Curator: Curated Learning for Self Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37651v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in multi-turn agentic workloads that require sustained interaction with complex environments. Existing reflective prompt optimizers such as GEPA learn from failed rollouts, but agentic failures can also come from prompt weaknesses, harness limitations, stochastic execution, or noisy judges. We argue that such failures may not provide useful learning signal, and may even be harmful; and thus it is essential to curate which tasks are provided to the prompt optimizer for better performance. We introduce GEPA-Curator, a framework that augments the prompt optimizer to select which agent experiences should shape prompt optimization. In particular, GEPA-Curator consists of two components: task curation, which filters out tasks that are likely to provide noisy or unreliable learning signals, and prior success retrieval, which selectively retrieves past successful trajectories for a task and provides them as additional context to the prompt optimizer. We evaluate GEPA-Curator on GoBrowse, ALFWorld, AppWorld, and $τ$-bench, using both Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5 as optimizer models. GEPA-Curator matches or exceeds the mean performance of both baselines, with gains of up to 20.7% over GEPA and 21.2% over ACE.</p></details> |  |

Specifically, we study how data composition in synthetically generated tool-use environments affects the training of such agents. To do so, we propose the SynthAPI pipeline, which explicitly controls the diversity of generated environments along the axes of tools, tasks, and the databases on which the tools operate. These predictable relationships reveal several lessons for scaling tool-use agent data. | **[Does Your Generalist Skill Optimizer Really Incentivize Agentic Tasks? A real LLM agent follows an injection that names a visible audit notice (generic, tool, and timing clauses have no effect); over that channel the plain certificate fails on measured labels and, by a post-hoc analysis, in a live run on a pre-registered schedule, while a silent audit restores it. As our language emerges out of our human communications, physical macroscopic order parameter emerges out of communications among atoms --- each atom is regarded as an agent who predicts each other's behavior through inference. We call the learned macroscopic coordinate the collective predictive order. Furthermore, for the ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic Ising models, the equilibrium distribution of the collective predictive order yields an emergent potential with the symmetry and thermal evolution characteristic of a Landau potential. Notably, the emergence of the collective predictive order does not refer to any structure of microscopic Hamiltonians/Lagrangians. Thus our ``Atom-as-an-Agent" (AaaA) principle offers a completely data-driven method for the emergence of macroscopic order, that is, machine learning physics.</p></details> | 33 pages, 10 figures |

## SFT
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[mSFT: Addressing Dataset Mixtures Overfitting Heterogeneously in Multi-task SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21606v7)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current language model training commonly applies multi-task Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) using a homogeneous compute budget across all sub-datasets. This approach is fundamentally sub-optimal: heterogeneous learning dynamics cause faster-learning tasks to overfit early while slower ones remain under-fitted. To address this, we introduce mSFT, an iterative, overfitting-aware search algorithm for multi-task data mixtures. mSFT trains the model on an active mixture, identifies and excludes the earliest overfitting sub-dataset, and reverts to that specific optimal checkpoint before continuing. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that mSFT consistently outperforms 4 baselines across 10 benchmarks and 6 base models. Further analysis confirms mSFT maintains robust gains across diverse dataset sizes, task granularities, and is insensitive to its single new hyperparameter (compute budget). Notably, at low compute budget, mSFT can improve performance while lowering training FLOPs. Ultimately, mSFT establishes a practical overfitting-aware algorithm for multi-task SFT that maximizes the potential of models across diverse data mixtures.</p></details> | AACL 2026 Oral |
| **[GRASP: Group-Adaptive Hybrid RL-SFT Post-Training for Medical VQA without Reasoning Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34561v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adapting general-purpose vision-language models (VLMs) to medical visual question answering (VQA) is valuable for helping clinicians read images and make diagnoses, but annotated data are scarce and provide only short answers, without reasoning annotations. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) fits these labels but overfits to them and amplifies their biases, eroding out-of-domain (OOD) generalization. Reinforcement learning (RL) better preserves generalization but never uses the gold answer as a training target: GRPO-style algorithms learn from the contrast between correct and incorrect answers within a group of sampled responses, so all-correct or all-wrong groups give no correctness signal. Existing SFT-RL hybrids either apply SFT and RL in separate stages or switch between them per question, or need reasoning traces from experts or stronger models, which medical VQA data lack. We propose GRASP, which combines RL and SFT in every update using only short answers: RL lets the model explore its own reasoning, SFT teaches the gold answer after that reasoning to supply the direct supervision RL lacks, and their balance adapts to how well the model answers each question. Tuning only about 1% of the parameters with LoRA, GRASP outperforms all compared training objectives both in domain and on OOD open-ended questions across Qwen3-VL-8B, InternVL3-8B and Gemma 3-4B. On an OOD benchmark spanning eight imaging modalities, GRASP-trained InternVL3-8B and Qwen3-VL-8B beat all 10 compared general and medical VLMs in open-ended accuracy, including models trained on up to 2,600 times more data or with over 250 times more trainable parameters. We release MedTerse, a dataset of 12.9K short-answer questions, and our code at https://github.com/sea-comet/GRASP-MedVQA.</p></details> |  |

Teachers and students share the same Qwen3.5-9B initialization, and the two teacher types are compared at similar task performance. | **[What Should SFT Learn? We study how to balance SFT and RL under production-mirroring beta APIs. We observe that, in our controlled experiment, checkpoint trajectories retrospectively separated into three regimes: Imitation, where SFT captured reliable teacher behavior; Lift, where both stages helped; and Discovery, where useful reward-observable behavior lay outside reliable teacher support. We leverage this prospectively, using teacher support and reward-observable headroom to route features to SFT only, SFT then RL, increased RL allocation, or further environment development. Across 18 subsequent feature-specific experiments, the diagnostic predicted 15/18 observed trajectories. On GPT-OSS 120B, targeted SFT then RL produced positive point estimates on 7/8 advertiser skills relative to a frontier Control; five positive gains had paired 95% confidence intervals excluding zero, while one skill had a confidence-supported regression. The largest gain was non-disclosure (+11.27 points; 95% CI [+9.72, +12.82]). | **[Arabic Safety Alignment as Selective Refusal: An Empirical Study of SFT, DPO, and Guard Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29378v1)** | 2026-08-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Arabic large language models must refuse harmful prompts without over-refusing benign or sensitive prompts, yet a single refusal rate hides this trade-off. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach not only provides superior SFT guidance but also exhibits robust performance and temporal scalability across increasing model sizes. This work transcends the fundamental limitation of traditional interpretability-its inability to identify task-critical mechanisms before they are trained-pioneering a predictive frontier that unites mechanistic interpretability with targeted optimization.</p></details> | 25 pages |
| **[Is Next-Chunk Reasoning RL Really Better than SFT? | **[Thinking in a Low-Resource Language: What SFT Builds, What RL Fixes, What Accuracy Cannot See](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17744v1)** | 2026-08-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Take three frontier mixture-of-experts models (Alibaba, OpenAI, NVIDIA; 3.6-4.0B active parameters each) and fine-tune them to reason in a low-resource language. On accuracy benchmarks almost nothing happens, and the benchmark itself is noise at this scale: changing only the random seed moves the score by 7.7 points, more than every data and recipe effect we measured. That null is our first result. The real changes live where accuracy cannot see. Base models never think in Greek: 0 of 1,000 reasoning traces, even when the question is Greek, so the model answers correctly while reasoning in a form its user cannot read, audit, or correct. After supervised fine-tuning (SFT), every released checkpoint reasons in the language of the question on ~98% of items, one family at 3x fewer tokens, with judged grammaticality improving on all four models and general ability within a few points of each base: nothing was forgotten, and fluency was gained. We propose six behavioural dimensions that make such changes measurable, each gated to reject any metric that correlates with output length, and we report how our own instruments lied: six failures, each caught by a control. What SFT cannot do is fix its own defects: a quarter of answers skip the requested format, answers leak into the reasoning channel, and an explicit "think in English" is obeyed under half the time. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, pre-registered before training, fixes the first two outright (fallback 24% to 2.5%, leak 3.5% to 0.0%, both against a flat random-reward control) and moves the third (+9.1pp), while the Greek reasoning habit survives an accuracy-only gradient untouched. | **[Rethinking Generalization in Reasoning SFT: A Conditional Analysis on Optimization, Data, and Model Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06628v2)** | 2026-08-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A prevailing narrative in LLM post-training holds that supervised finetuning (SFT) memorizes while reinforcement learning (RL) generalizes. | **[Fine-Tuning Qwen3-27B for C-to-Rust Code Translation: A Three-Stage Curriculum of Pretraining, Debugging-Aware SFT, and Task-Specific SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13681v1)** | 2026-08-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Translating C code into safe, idiomatic Rust is a longstanding software-engineering goal because it can eliminate entire classes of memory-safety vulnerabilities while preserving the functional behavior of legacy systems. | **[How Much Backtracking is Enough? Exploring the Interplay of SFT and RL in Enhancing LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24273v2)** | 2026-08-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) suggest that reinforcement learning (RL) effectively internalizes search strategies, yielding significant improvements on challenging reasoning tasks through extended chains of thought. We motivate this claim by first studying the interplay between supervised fine-tuning (SFT) warm-up and RL across eight reasoning tasks: Countdown, Sudoku, Arc 1D, Advanced Geometry, Color Cube Rotation, List Functions, Zebra Puzzles, and Self Reference. We find that a regular SFT warm-up using self-sampled CoT sequences provides a modest boost to subsequent RL compared to training without any SFT warm-up; however, such gains saturate as tasks become more difficult. Motivated by this failure mode, we introduce a backtracking-centric training recipe. By synthetically varying the number of explicit backtracking steps in the SFT warm-up, we show that (i) longer CoTs containing backtracks stabilize and amplify RL, and (ii) the optimal backtrack depth scales with task difficulty: zero for Arc 1D, one for Countdown, and five or more for Sudoku. We first analyze the learning dynamics of RL fine-tuning with verifiable process rewards and SFT in a unified way, allowing us to identify sufficient conditions under which the transformer provably learns these functions. We then verify that the conditions hold for three examples, including $k$-PARITY, $k$-AND, and $k$-OR, thus demonstrating their learnability via both RL and SFT. Notably, we reveal that RL and SFT exhibit distinct learning behaviors depending on supervision: RL learns the whole CoT chain simultaneously, whereas SFT without teacher forcing learns the CoT step-by-step. Overall, our findings provide insights on the mechanisms underlying RL and SFT and how they differ in triggering the CoT capabilities of transformers, and suggest that the comparison between RL and SFT should consider the intermediate supervision.</p></details> | <details><summary>ICML ...</summary><p>ICML 2026 final version. 50 pages</p></details> |
| **[SFT Conflicts, RL Coexists: A Theoretical and Empirical Analysis of Multi-Task Learning for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03573v2)** | 2026-08-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) exhibit fundamentally different behaviors in enhancing multi-task reasoning for large language models (LLMs). Our results reveal a distinction: interference in SFT is norm-limited, scaling with the absolute gradient magnitude, whereas interference in RL is variance-limited, bounded by the gradient variance induced by advantage normalization and on-policy optimization. | **[Similar Models Learn Differently: Final-Window Pretraining Shapes Post-Training Beyond SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25063v1)** | 2026-07-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developers judge a model checkpoint by how it behaves. SFT and post-training are then identical. After SFT the branches behave near-identically, within about one point on instruction following, refusal, and capability, yet the same post-training carries them to very different endpoints, under both a direct preference optimization update and a reinforcement learning update with a verifiable reward. The other four branches gain little or no protection, so the effect is selective to what the window contained. The protection requires the safety text to arrive last rather than earlier in pretraining, and it reproduces on a second model family. What a model is pretrained on last shapes how it reacts to alignment. | **[SFGA: A Statistics-First Gating Architecture with Adjudicative Escalation for Trustworthy SFT Data Procurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18960v1)** | 2026-07-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Procuring supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data forces a buyer to decide, before any downstream training, whether a candidate corpus is worth acquiring. We further report honest negative diagnostics of the debate path: a con-side win rate of 0.80 ($p\approx3{\times}10^{-6}$) and a 52\% position-flip rate under advocate swapping expose negativity and positional biases that a naive LLM-judge would hide. Extensive experiments on general STEM and mathematical benchmarks demonstrate that our decoupled curriculum training significantly outperforms SFT-only, RL-only, and random-split baselines.
